1To make Harts horne Gelly an
appoued good way

Take some 4 . ounces of scraped Harts horne, and lay it a steepe in
ale quart of faire running water 24 houres, then put in a pipin
cut in pices, and if the party be cold, you may put in neare a pinte
of shery sack, a large mace, half a nutmegg;  4 or 5 cloues, and
a few Coliander seeds, these tye in a little linnen ragg and a
slice of leom with it, and you must put in neere halfe a pinte
of red rose water, this must be boiled softly, with a soberfire
untill it be halfe consumed then put in halfe a pound of the
best loofe suger, or 3 quarters, as the party likes it or as the
substance of the gelly requireth. when it is boiled enough,
which you shall know, by setting some a cooling in a spoone
for you must not boile it soe long till it button on the side
of a dish, for then it will be tough, when you thinke it enoigh
put in a branch of rosemary into your cotten Gelly bagg, and let
it runne into your gelly pot or glasse, This will keepe as well as
Conserue, and one spoonefull will nourish as much as a
whole Cock it is good in Consumption & against the wormes
it Carrieth many euills from the stomack and the heart
you may eate of it att any time or take it in broth, you may
make it of Bugglasse watter the more to comfort the heart if
one be to hot take Endiue water, if ouer stufft take hysop
water or fennell water

2To make China Broth

Take an ounce of the best china, slice it thin, of yellow sanders
sliced, the weight of 4, steep this in 3. quarts of Barley
water, for 26. houres, then with an old Cock drest and the
bones broken, of Burrage, sidrach, Coltsfoot and harts tongue
leaues of each a handfull, of Rosemary, Maidenhaire and
time, of each halfe a handfull, Raisins of the sunne stoned,
a large handfull, 6. figgs sliced, as many dates, a large mace,
half a handfull of white poppie seed, and att the latter end
of boiling, put in a quarter of a pinte of muscadine, boile all
to the consumption, of halfe the licor or better, either for
broth or Gelly.

3To make Second Pottage.

Take a quart of the thickest and sweetest rawe creame, then sweeten
it uery well with sugar, and put in . 6 spoonesfull of rose water
then take . 6 Eggs, and put away 4 of the whites then beat them
well together, when the creame is well boiled with the sugar and
rose water, put them in and stirre them together, when it is a little
more boiled, take it off and powre it into a cleane cloth, that is not
too fine, and tie it together and hang it upm and when it is throughly
cold and drained, dish it but first beate it a little together ere you
serue it





4To make a fine Northfolk
dumplin

Take 3 . Eggs, half a pint of good milke . 3 great spoonsfull of
flower, and a little salt, stirre them well together, and take as
thick a cloth as you can get, fil tie it up quickly and put it in a
seething pot, when it is sodd, take butter, a spoonefull of rosewater
and a little suger, & melt it together, to powre ouer your Dumplin

5To make a Custard

Take a pint of good Creame and boile it leasurely a little while,
putting therein Carrawaye seeds if you like it, then take the yolkes
of 7 Eggs, and beat them well, then put in your Eggs with a spoone
full or two of rosewater, Cinamon, ginger, cloues, and mace pounded
sweetening it with suger, and soe put it in to a pewter dish
ouer a pot of water, letting it stand the space of an houre
couering it with an other dish, and wiping the steeme from that
dish now and then

6To make a Tansy

Take . 5 or . 6 Eggs, and take out the white of one Egge, put therto
halfe a pinte of Creame and better, and a spoonefull of
flower, the Juice of tansy, sorrell, uiolet leaues, straweberry
leaues, beat all these well together and straine them, put to
it cloues & mace soe fry it with sweet butterm not forgetting salt.

7To make Dyet bread

Take a pound of your finest flower, sersed, a pound of the finest sugar
beaten, and well searsed, one ounce of carway seed, a nounce of aniseed, an
ounce of sweet fenell seed, your seeds must be well dryed and rubbed
betweene your hands, and searsed, x Eggs beaten together with the
sugar and seeds, after that take the flower aboue named, and let
one uery softly shake it into the Egges, suger and seeds, and other
still stirring till all the flower be in them, the batter must be
presently put into the coffins, and the Coffins into the ouen
and soe the bread shall be soone baked the coffins must
be made of oake, and the bottoms of plate, and before the batter
be put in your must butter the coffins

8To make Puffe Paste

Take a quart of fine flower finely fitted . 2 eggs, whit and all
halfe a pounde of batter, halfe a spoonefull of rosewater, and
as much sugar, first mingle the flower & the eggs with a little
peece of butter, and make it uery stiff, then slice it in thin slices
and lay a piece of butter betwene each of them and role them
with a rolling pinne, soe let them lie a while before you put
them in fashion





70
8To make little fried puddings.

Grate a penie white loafe, take a quarter of a pound of currants
3 egges and a pretty quantity of suet, a little spoonefull of
sugar, a little spice, salt, make it a little wtt with milke, soe
that it may be taken up with a spoone and laid in the panne
by spoonsfull, soe fry them with sweet butter.

9To make Hoggs puddings white

Take . 6 . pennyworth of manchet and grate it, and put to it 3.
of hoggs suet, cut uery small, but first season it with a little
pepper some cloues and mace, one ounce of nutmeggs, grated
and a little fennell bruised . xu . Eggs and but 4 of the whits
a little rosewater sprinckled upon it, 3  of Currants washed
then boile half a hoggs liuer readie and while it is hot
stamp it and straine it with warame milke, and put it
in with a little salt, and stirre all well together, and wett
it with more warme milke till it be uery lithey, & fill
them with a fillbowle and boile them somewhat fast.

11To make Bloudinges

Take greate whole oatmeale and bruise it uery little, steepe
it all night in scalding milke then mingle it with a
little bloud not to much and put in a little beffe broth
and some hoggs suet cut in great peeces, cloues & mace
pepper and fennell seed a little

12To make all sorts of
cordiall Conserues

Take the flowers of Burrage, or Buglosse, succory or Endife
in quantity 4 ounces, and adde thereto of rose water one
pinte boile this on a soft fire of charcoals till halfe
of the water be consumed then add thereto of the purest
loafe sugar 12 ounces, and boile it to the forme of a gelly
reseruing it for cooling conserues and cordiall to the heart

13To make Cordiall in
weakenes

Take of Eringo rootes . 2 ounces, of orenge pitts & citrons
pitts of each 2 ounces, of Coliander comfits, and anisseeds
comfits, of Each . 2 1/2 ounces, of the powder of Ploriser
centicon a quarter of an ounce of pearle prepared as
much, of amber greece 20 graines of fine sugar . 6 ouces
of red rose water as much as will boile the sugar to the
hight of manus christi, you must first boile your rosewater
and sugar to that height and then put in your roots and pitts
being maruellous finely sliced, and softly boile it and then
gently shake in your other powders, and giue it a gentle whalme
and soe make them into Cakes





14 The best way to make Conserue
of Red roses

Take 3 pintes of runing water to halfe a pinte of roses & . 2 pounds
of suger the boile the water and let it coole againe, then put
in your roses unbeaten but the white cut away as you pull them
from the budd before you use them, soe let them boile till you
perceiue the leaues to be tender and come to there colour, then put
in your sugar and boile them up, and soe pot them for your use

15An other way

Take Roses before they be too much blowne and cut off the whites of them
and to euery ounce of roses, put . 2 . ounces of sugar and soe beate
them together in a morter till they be uery small, then put them
in a glasse, closely couering them, and set them in the sunne when
it is uery hot 4 or 5 dayes

16How to make conserue of Barberies

Take an earthen pott and put your barberies in it and let them stewe
ouer the fire, the pott being closely sopt, then straine them out, puting
to euery pound thereof a pound of sugar and mingel well alltogether
and let it boile till it be thick

17An other approued way of the
same

18To keepe Barberies like perserued
ones all the yeare

Take your Barberies well growne and stone them laying a rowe of
Barberies, cast sugar on them and then a row of Barberies and sugar
againe, to pott them up in your gelly potts for the whole yeares use

19To keepe Barberies for garnishing
of Dishes

Make a brie of water and salt, and let it stand till it be cold, and
put in your barberies and they will keepe good

20For to Candy flowers for sallets

Take your flowers and their weight in sugar boile your sugar to a Candy
height, then put in the flowers as it boileth, then dry them in the
dish and keepe them to your use





71
21To keepe Cowcombers all the yeare

Take vinegar and a little water and salt, make a pickle thereof, &
let it stand till it be cold, and then put in your cowcombers and
soe keepe them all the yeare

22To keepe Gooseberries all the yeare

Take vinegar and a little salt and stirre it and then put in your
Gooseberies wel growne and greene, and soe keepe them

23To make Cakes

Take a peck of flower, 4  of butter 4  of currants, one pinte of
water to the butter, as soone as it is melted, use it, for it must not
be too hot, one pinte of strong ale barme, halfe a pound of sugar
of mace and cloues two pennyworth, 2 nutmeggs, an ounce of
ginger, 3 pennyworth of saffran, wett the saffron in 6 spoones
full of rosewater, take heed you season your ouen well, and if
they take lay paper

To make Almond Butter

Take one pound of Jordain allmonds the best and blanch them, then
beate them in a stone morter, with some fair conduit water, then
when they are well beaten straine them out and beat them
and straine them out 3 or 4 times with other faire water til
the water and beating hath drawne out all the substance : then
set the milke ouer the fire, in a skillet or panne, and when it
begins to rise up in the boiling, take the Juice of a lemmon
cleane from the kernels and powre it into the milke stirring
it together, when it beginneth to rise againe take it up, and
let some hold a broad & faire holland cloth, and power it abroad
upon that cloth, with a spoone or a ladle to the end that
the whey may runne cleane from it, then let them hold
hard, and scrape all the whey from the underside of the cloth
then put all the substance that is upon the cloth together
with the edge of a spoone, then put the cloth together and
tye it close together with a string and let it hang up all
night by that string to draine, then in the morning take some
uery fine sugar a quarter of a pound, and . 6 spoonesfull of
rosewater, and soe sweeten it for your use

To make marchpaine to Ice it guilt it, and
garnish it, according to the arte of a Confit
maker

Take allmonds and put them into seething water, then blanch them
upon a dry cloth and beate them in a morter, now and then
dropping in a drop or two of rosewater, to keepe them from oyling
now and then throwing in a handfull of searsed sugar, and when
you have brought it to a perfect paste, then rowling it abroade,
cut it round by a dish or a plate, as bigg as you will haue it, then
set an edge about it as high as about a Tarte, soe set it into the
ouen as hot as for manchet, and when it is halfe baked and more
then take rosewater & sugar, & a little of the white of an egge &
beate it together with a spoone, spreade this upon it with a feather, when
it is well Iced, take it out of the ouen, & garnish it with whatsoeuer you will
the conceipts must be made first stick it full with long Comfits strew biskets
& carawayes upon it, & when it is cold guilt it and serue it.





26To make Conceipts to print with
mouldes

Take some of your marchpaine paste and roule it something thinne, &
take wooden mouldes without brimmes and a little rub the sugar ouer
the paste, but strew none into the moulde

27To make Italian Bisket

Tak searsed sugar and beate it in an Allabaster morter with Gume
Traganth and the white of an egge, a little muske, a little ambergreese
then moulde it up with a little anisseed being cleane dusted then mould
it in the bignes of a crab, cut it about like a manchet, & prick it quite
through in the top, then set them in a reaosnable quick ouen, and
when they are rison somewhat high, take them out and remoue them
not till they be cold, you must take them upon a pie plate

28To make prime Bisket

Take sugar finely searsed one pound, and a pound of fine flower
8 . Eggs whereof two you must take out the yolkes, & beate them an
houre together, then take coffins of tinne, & rub them with butter,
then heate the ouen as for manchet, when you are readie to fill
the coffins halfe full and better, & set them into the ouen

29To make Collips of paste like
Bacon

Take some of your marchpaine paste, & mould it in saunders finely
searsed untill it be red, then rowle out 3 . cakes of red and . 4 of white
and lay them one upon another, a white and a red then cut them
ouerfwart in slices like Collips of Bacon then m rowle them
a little not uery thinne, then lay them on a warme stone &
dry them

30 To make Eggs to your Collips

Take sugar finely searsed, worke it up to paste with a gumme
traganth being steept in rose water staine some of this paste
yellow with saffron, then rowle it up round like the yolkes
of Eggs & fashion the whites with your hands like an Egge
then cut it in 4 quarters and with a little gumme traganth
wett a quarter & set it on the yellow which will look like
a Egge cut in 4 quarters

31To make Diastoma, which are
otherwise called cleare cakes

Take Rasberries and put them in a stone Jugge, & set the
Jugge into a pot of seething water and euer as they doe dis-
solue powre the liquor through a strainer into a Bason,
then take a pinte of the liquor & a pound of sugar, &
put the said liquor into a posnet, & the sugar into an
other and put as much water to the sugar as will melt
it, then boile it till it come to a candy height and boile
the liquor as long in the other posnet, then put them seething
hot together, and boile them a little while then powre it
hot





72
hot into glasses being made like marmelet Boxes,
so set them in a warme ouen or stoue, and let
them stand one weeke, then turne them and set
them in againe an other weeke, till they be drye,
allways keeping them with a gentle heate, &
they will looke as cleare as Quidinee, and a
hard fee candid on the other side of them, that
a Gentilwoman may carrie it in her pocket
this you may make of any tender plume or
berry

32To make Italia Chips

Take fine sugar finely searsed and uiolet sugar & rose sugar
finely searsed, and make them up to paste, euery one of them
seuerally with Gumme traganth steept in rosewater, then
towle them uery thinne and lay them one upon an other
then rowle them up like a quier of paper & cut them
ouerthwart in clices and rowle them uery thinne

33To make Jumbolles

Take a pound of the finest flower, & a pound of hard
sugar uery small beatten and beate them together
then take an ounce of anisseed well dusted and
bruised in a morter and halfe an ounce of Carraways
seeds, halfe an ounce of Colliander seedes prepared,
by laying them an houre into whitewine uinegar, take
the uineger cleane from the seeds and dry it by
the fier then take all these seeds, and strew them
amongst the flower, then take 8 Eggs putting
away . 4 . of the whites and mingle them together,
then worke them in rowles and knott them and
set them upon papors and prick them, and set
them in the ouen heate as before, and when these
are well baked put them up

34To make mackmacharoones

Take a pound of Jordaine Allmondes & a pint of fine
allmonds like sugar then lay them in faire water
and blanch them, and beate them in an Allabaster
morter or other stone morter, and as you beate them put
in now and then some rosewater, soe continuing till they
be uery finely beaten and strewe in amongst the Almonds
now and then your sugar apound of it, which must be
uery small and fine, beaten together till they bee well
sprigs incorporated, then put all out into a dish, and take the
butchers whites of 2 eggs, and beate them with . 3 . or . 4 butchers spriggs
broome and as the froth ariseth stirre it in amongst the allmonds
then sett the dish upon a chaffing dish with coales,
stirring the ALmmonds continually for feare of burning, then
when it is throughly hot, take it from the coales, then
hauing wafers cut long or round as you please, put
your stuffe upon them, and set them in the ouen on
paper after household bread, or uery well warmed with a
couple of small fagotts, leauing the ouens mouth open
and when they are sufficiently drye, take them out, and
if you loue muske you may put in a little in the beating
of them with the rose water.
An





35An other way to make Mackaroones

Take one pound of sugar fine beaten, one pound of Allmonds blanched
as you blanch them put them into cold water, when they be throughly
cold, drye them in a linnen cloth and as you beat them sprinkle
now and then a sponefull of rosewater into them you may if you
please, steepe a graine of muske or Ambergrees in rosewater, six
spoonesfull will be enough to moisten the whole pound, when the
Allmonds are throughly beaten mingel your suger, then take 3 .
Eggs . 2 . of the yolkes being taken away, mingle it all well together
then ley them out thick upon marchpaine sheetes, and bake
them upon pie plates

36To make Puffe Paste.

37To make marrow pudding .

Take a penny white loafe and slice it round & thinn,
annoint the dish with butter and strew a few currants,
then rubbe cinamon and sugar, in both sides of the bread
and couer the dish with the bread then lay marrow on the
bread in pretty bigg pieces all ouer, then quarter 4 dates
and lay them upon the marrow, & strewe currants, cinamon
and sugar upon the bread and dates, and doe so till your
dish bee full, but lay bread uppermost and strewe currants
uery thick upon it, then take 3 yelkes of Eggs and halfe
a pint of creame and a little rosewater sweeten it with sugar
and power upon it till the dish be full, then let it bale
uery gently and serue it in with sugar ouer it

38To refine sugar

Take a poud of sugar & halfe a pint of water & boile them
together till all the sugar be melted, then take the white
of an Egge and faire water . 12 . spoonesfull, and beate
them together with a brushing rod till they come to froth
then put to it the hard sugar, and set it on the fire againe,
and uer as it riseth, drop in a drop of cold water, then
let it boile, till you see the froth of the sugar rise as
whit as an Egge and then take it by the fire & scumme it





74
39To boile sugaer to a Candy
hight to Cast into moulds

Boile your sugar till it will fly as fine as the
haire of your head

40To preserue Gooseberries

Take them being full growne and greene, then open
the topes of them, and take out some of the kernells
with a bodkine, then make your syrop weight for
weight, putting . 5 . or 6 . spoonesfull of water unto one
pound of sugar, diuiding your pound of gooseberries
into . 3 parts, and boile them gently, till they
begin to be cleare shaking them in the panne or
skillet without a spoone, then boile them up
quick and soe put them forth into a dish till they
be cold, then put them up.

41To preserue Cherries white

Make the Syrop Candy height, then take your
Cherries being full ripe, and pill them and dip
them into that Syrop scolding hot, as you pill them
and lay them into a cleane dish one by one, and
when you haue boiled as many as you thinke fitt
boile them up quick with more sugar

42To preserue Cherries red

Take your fairest cherries and stone them leauing
on them part of the stalke, then weigh them weight
for weight with beaten sugar, then put in about
a dozen cherries in a pound ouer and about your
weight, then strow some sugar in your panne &
set your cherries one by one in that sugar, and soe
a laying of cherries and one of sugar, then put into
it about . 3 spoonesfull of faire water and set them
upon a gentle fire, and take heed of burning them
and soe let them boile till the sugar hath purchased
the colour of the cherries and come to their owne
colour againe, then boile them up as fast as you
can till you see them buble up with broade bubles
so take them off and set them to coole, till, you
pot them.

43To preserue Quinces Red

Weight your Quinces with your sugar weight for
weight and take a pinte of water to them, that is
to euery pound of sugar, then coare and [are them
and boile them together till they come red close
and turne them often, for feare of spotting, let
them boile till they be uery tender, then take
them up, if your syrop be not thick enough to pearle
stiffe upon a plate, let it boile longer & soe powre
them together
to





44To presereue Quinces white

Take a pound of Quinces, as much sugar, then cut
out a pound peece in the top, and coare them, &
stope them close with theire own stopple,
boile them in conduit water uncouered, till
they be halfe boiled, then take them up, and
tope them - and pare them, soe put them
into a pale of faire conduit water, in the meane
time make your syrop with one pinte of runing
water to a pound of sugar, then wipe the water
off with a cleane cloth, and your syrop being
halfe boiled, put them in and boile them up as
fast as you can uncouered till they bee uery
tender turning them sometimes for feare
of breaking, when they are sufficiently boiled
let them stand in a basin till they be throughly
cold, then put them up and stop them close.

45To make Red Marmelet

Take Quinces and bake them, and stope the pot
you put them in, then pare them and scrape all
the pappe from the coare, then take the pappe
and beate it in a morter with a back of a spoone
then take as much sugar, as it will weigh, and
boile it to a candy height with as much water as
will melt it, then put the papper into the hot sugar
and boile it, still stirring it till it will come cleane
from the bottome of the panne then put it in your
boxes or glasses, smoothing it ouer, and soe set it
on a warme stone to dry it, soe you may keepe it all
the yeare

46To make Marmelet of Quinces
White

Take Quinces and boile them in there owne skinne
and when they be cold pare them, and scrape all
the pappe from the coare, then take as much
sugar as the papper will weigh, but your papper
must be strained through a peece of Caskon Canvas
then boile your sugar to a Candy height, then put
your papper into your hot sugar and boile it with
stirring till it will come cleane from the bottome
of the panne, and soe box it & keepe it all the yeare

47To Make Marmelet of oranges
and Lemons

Take orenges and boile them tender shifting them
8. or . 9 times in the boiling then beate them in a
wodden bowle and straine them through a piece of
thine canvas, soe boile them with stirring till it
come cleane from the panne, and soe box it ...
the first water you boile them in you must put in
a handfull of white salt and it will preserue theire
colour.
To





75
48To preserue Plummes or any other
growne fruit

Take white plumes when they are uery greene and
scald them moderatly till they doe peele, then
boile them soberly, in cold water againe till they
waxe greene, take one pound and a quarter of sugar
to a pound of plumes then make your syrop as
you doe for other plummes, and let them boile in
it leisuarly till they be readie, and after the same
manner you may use greene Apples or other greene
fruite

49To make greene fruit
paste

You must take the greene outside of the fruit after
it is peeled and scalded, then beate it uery fine
and put it in your syrop made Candy height, then
boile them softly till they will stand then put
them into a warme ouen or stoue, & dry them lesurly

50To preserue Apricockes
Peaches & pare plummes

Take your plummes now gathered, and as much sugar
as they way weigh, and as much water as to make a
syrop to couer them when your sugar is boiled let
it stand & coole a while, then put in your plummes
for if it be hot they will cracke, soe let them stand in
that syrop all the night keeping it allways warme
and noe more, and on the morrow set your balson
uppon a pot of seething water and soe proserue
them uery leisurely, putting in a little more
dry sugar then they has at first and when they
are uery tender & sweat then take them out and
boile the syrop till it will button uppon a dish side
and when they are both coold put them up together
this way wee preserue all plumes and berries as
Redberries, Gooseberries or currantes

51To drye Apricockes, peares
or plumes, either naturall
or artificiall

Take your Aprickocks or peare plumes & presarue
them first, stone your Apprcockes but doe not presarue
them soe tenderly in Syrop as to keepe them, wash
all the Syrop off of the one side with warme water
then strewe searsed sugar on them, till you couer them
ouer, then lay them uppon paper and set them upon
a fine bottome in a warme ouen, and euery day
turning of them now and then strewe searsed
sugar on them, till they be dry then boxe them
To





52To drye them naturall

Take the Plumes, and lay them all night in sweet wort
and on the morrow lay them on a pie plate, and halfe
bake them, and then set them into the ouer with a
gentle heate, soe day by day till they be dry, soe you
may boile them and keepe them all the yeare, in this
like sorte you may pippins or peares, your Apricockes
will looke as, cleare as amber.

53To make Paste of Orenges
and Lemons

Take orenges and lemons and boile them in spring
water, shift them a dozen times in the boiling out
of boiling water into boiling water, to the first water
put in a handfull of salt, to . 2 . or . 3 orenges that
will preserue the colour and keepe them from
shrinking, take as much sugar as they doe weigh
refine it & scume it cleane then straine the papper
of an orenge or lemon in a thin caskon canuas,
then put the papper into the hot sugar, being
boiled of a candy height, then boile it with stirring
and fashion it as you please

54To Candy Cleare Rock Candy
roots, fruites spices or flowers

Take fruit or rootes and preserue them & dry
them againe, take spices and flowers and boile them
in a syrop of sugar, till it be sugar againe then take
them and spread them upon a sheate of paper then
lay them uppon round wiers, in an earthen panne
then take as much sugar as will fill your panne
and refine it, and boile it to a Candy height then
powre it into your panne amongest your fruits and
flowers, then spot it in a Chemney corner upon
a Cushion and couer it with a blancket foure
double, and soe let it stand warme to dry an houre
after the Syrop is out of it, then picke by the wyers
and take off the fruits and lay them uppon a
paper, then set . 2 or . 3 houres before the fire
to drie, then box it and keepe it all the yeare

55To Candy Aringoe rootes.

Take Aringoe rootes, new gathered smooth
without Jointes and boile them in spring water
the water boyling before you put then in, then
boile them as a parsenep, then slit them off
the way cleane throughout, and take the pith out
of them, & peele them then wash them in many
waters milke warme then dry them uppon a
linnen cloth and playte them 3 . and 3 together
tyed with a threed together, att both endes, then
take as much sugar as they weigh, and as much water
as will make a Syrop to couer them, halfe of it Rose
water and the other, cleane water, then refine the sugar
and.





76
and soe put the water into your hot sugar boiling it together
stiring it till they haue drunke up all the sugar then laye
them uppon paper, and drie them, and box them & they will
keepe all the yeare,

56To Candy the Spanish Candy

Take the blossomes of flowers and bruise them not, but
boile them in sugar till it be soe thick that it will scarce
drope frome the spoone, then powre some of it uppon a
wett trencher and with a wette knife spreade it resionable
thine when it is cold, cut it square like Diamonds, that which
you leaue in the posenet and the Cuttings off, stirr and bruise
with the back of a spoone, and vuery leafe will part from the
other

59 To Preserue Damsons
Take of the bruised and least damsons and put them into
a skillett of faire water, and boile them till they come
to their colour, then weigh of the fairest allmost weight
for weight with sugar: then lay a laying of sugar and a
laying of damsons in the skillett and power into them
a quarter of a pint of the liquor, and soe set them
ouer the fier and let them simper very tenderly, till
they purchasse their colour and be very tender, then
set them away all night in their liquor, and next morning
boile them vp till the syrop will button vpon the side of
a dish still being carefull to take out those that be
tender and ready, then putting all those that you haue
taken out giue them a gentle whalme together & when
they be cold pott them

60 The best way to presarue all sorts
of fruit dry or wett
ffirst to make Cleare Cakes of
Gooseberries or any fruites or
Plumes
Take your fruit and put them into a stone pot and couer it
and put the said stone pot into a pot of seething water and
as the fruit doth boile tender power away the liquid substance
from them into a siluer dish, or fro Rasberries or English
Currants if uoy you will you may put them into a posnet
and soe boile them and stirre them till they be boiled all
to pices and put them into a strainer and let the liquor
runne into a siluer dish or gelly pot, being in hot water
whereby the liquor may be keept warme while it is a dropping
then the colour will be the better, then hauing an other dish
iust of the same weight of that which hath the liquor of your
fruit, weigh that liquor in the one dish and the sugar
in the other, and take Iust the weight of the liquor in fine
sugar, then brake the said sugar into small peeces and boile
it to a Candy height, then take the liquor of the fruit being
made seething hot and put it into your hot sugar and stirre it
togeather and soe let it stand from the fire, for it must
not boile in any case after the liquor is in the sugar, and
betweene hot and cold powre it into your moulds or glasses
made like marmalet boxes, and set them into a warme
stoue and soe let it stand till it be canded on the top, then
turne it on a peece of glasse & set them into the
worme stoue againe & soe let them stand there till
they be dryed throughly, and then burne them and set
them vp./

To presarue Pome Citrons after
  ?1 My Lady Hayes fashion, after
Christmasse
Take those that are pale and smooth rinded, quarter them them
cut that out where the Iuice lyes, then take your preseruing
panne full of cleane water sett it ouer the fier, and assoone
as it beginneth to boile, put in your quarters of fruit, make them
boile softly & doppe them downe with a silver spoone or stick
in the time thats boiling take on other panne of water and set
it ouer the fier, and assoone as it begins to boile, take your
fruit out of the first water, and put them into that pan
let them boile leissurely till they be tender take them out
and spreade them vpon a Cleane Cloath to drye when they
are cold weigh them and to euery pound of them weigh a
pound of loafe sugar, and a pinte and a halfe of water
or more, then put your sugar and water together in a clean
pan ouer the fier, and let it boile about a quarter of an houre
when it is well scumed put in your fruit, boile them softly a
little while, then take off your pan, and let it stand a little
set it ouer the fire againe, and make them boile a quarter
of an houre or more take them out and put them into gelly
pots syrop and all, so when they are cold Couer them with
papers and let them stand .3. dayes, after that put your
syrup in the pan, and add to it, and as much sugar as you put att
the first, boile it a quarte rof an houre, then take off your
panne, and let it stand as long, doe soe 3 times till the s
syrop be pretty thick, put them into your potts as before,
when they be cold couer them let them stand 8 dayes, power
them into the panne boile them a little while. take them off
one 3 times as before and pott them, when they are cold couer them.

qu'il ne ?itts ne iette pas nit plus d'escurie puis vous mettres   vos bettes

A: Salve for an old Sore
Take valerian. red sage. settwell. adders tongue. whitewort rock
et. leaves. spearemints. water betony. hearbe robert. of each a
handefull. stampe them alltogether. then take hoggs liquor
and yellow waxe. and rosen of each of these halfe a pounde
then put them tathe hearbes and sett them on a soft fire
and lett them boyle a reason able time some halfe howre
then take halfe an ounce of vnwashed turpentine and put
it in. when you are readye to take it of the fire let it stande
till it be colde. and make it vp in rowles. as you se cause
The powder that must be vsed with itt
Take of the finest white sugar. burnt allom. rosen beaten
to powder. of each a quantity alike. when the wound is cleansed
from the Coruption strew this vppon it and then lay in the salve
Spreade on a playster. If the wounde be not deepe. If it
be deepe mingle the salve with lint and soe put it in/
The Salve: H
Take a good quantity of wormewood soe much as will make
the Salve to looke Green stampe it very small. then
take a pound of bores grease that hath been melted and
Strayned. and soe much sheeps suett (as being well shred
and beaten small in a morter and then melted) will serve
some what to stiffen the salve and when the sheepes sewet
is Melted. put the wormewood vnto it. and lett it seeth for
a little space. and then put in the bores grease. lett all these
Seeth very well together. and when it is well boyled
put it in a strong course Nen Canvis & strayne and crush
it very hard. till all the liquor or Inice comes out into a
pot. then put in it a little sallet oyle and Stirr and mingle
it all together till it comes to a salve:/

if neede be

To Make a very proved broth
ointment against the gangrene

howres. and soe you shall finde that the mortifyed flesh
shall Separate round about from the good and hinder
the mortification to goe any further afterwarde
you may proceede in the cure with ordinarie remedie
to mundifie the flesh and heale it.

Harthorne Jelly

Take a pound of hartshorne &[and] 2 Gallons &[and] halfe
of spring Water, &[and] let it boyle over a soft fire
uncovered 4 hours, thn take a littl of it onto a plate
&[and] lett it cool" to see if it will jelly or not if it doth
nott thn lett it boyle till it doth, &[and] wn it is boyled enough
take it off, &[and] lett it cool for half a quarter ot of an houre
thn stran ir through a fibne sieve soe lett ir stand till
it be cold, wn it is cold sett it over th fire again, thn putt
in a pinte of th best white wine thn take th white of 8:
eggs beaten thm up to a froth, &[and] th juice of 6 lemonds put
into th white of th eggs, whn th jelly id upon th fir put
in 2 pound of th best refine sugar thn wh sugar is melted
put in th lemon &[and] eggs sturr it aboute till it just
boyle up thn take it of th fire 7 [and] run it through a jelly
bagg into glasses or wt you please.

A Medicine for hardeness of the belly
and stopping of splene. and liver and wormes
in Children
Take a quart of Strongest ale or strongest beere. boyle in this
of wormewood. a handefull. of Cardus benidictus of Contory
of St. Johns wort of each one handefull being Choped. and
boyle them in the ale or beere. with halfe a pound of fresh
butter. being well boyled. strayne this out very harde. and
with this liquor rub the belly. and sides downeward
before a fire. Morneing and evening:

To Purge Cholor and Melancholy
Syrrop of Some flowers infused a dozen times distilled
water. Sip Spoone fulls to an old body. in the morning
taken either alone or in a little mace ale purgeth
Cholor and melancholly.
Doctorr Matthias
Agaist a Malignant feaver
or agne
Pimpernell stilled in an ordinary still. to the quantity of a gallon. save the stilled hearbs and breake them small
and steepe them in that sue stilled. and putt
them in a stone pott. well stoped and lett them stand
over a bakers Chimny for a whole month. then ~
press the water Cleane from the hearbs againe and
still the water over agayne in a still and when the
water is allmost stilled out save the last that
remayne in the bottome of the still which will bee
somewhat thick and put it in a glass by it selfe and
putt sugar to it this water is a very speciall thing in
any malignant feaver or agne and the better if you
put as much as the quantity of a Nuttmegg of that
thick that was left in the bottome of the still as
before is mentioned

A: Blacke Salve for a Sprayne
or old Sore or New.
Take a pinte of Sallot oyle halfe a pound of red leade and
mix them well together. take an ounce and and a halfe of yellow
waxe and Slice it thin. Sett all these together on a fire
lett them boyle softly till the waxe be melted. then take
an ounce of spermaceti putt it vnto the rest. and lett it
boyle vntill it be black and rope be tweene yourr fingers. keepe
it with stirring for feare it run over. when it is boyled put
it in an Earthen pott to keepe it for r use if you will you may
add Camphire.
An Approved Medicine to breake
a fellon or pin rush.
Take a penny white leafe. Grate itt & boyle itt in Good wine vinegar
and in the boyleing putt in 4 spoonefulls of honny and halfe a
spoonefull of baysalt and soe boyle it to a poultise & spread itt uppon
Leather, & lay it soe far as is Swolne and when it is broken apply
thee salve before mentioned to the broken place. and then layye
poultise on itt.
A Remedy for the Cough of
the Lungues
Take a pottle of running water put to it halfe a pound of fyigs
slitt halfe a pound of Raysons of the summ and store themm halfe
a handefull of lignorice sliced thin. a spoone full of anniseedes
halfe a pound of french barley. fanned cleane. 3 of saxifrage
3 handfulls of Coltes foote a handfull and a halfe of Asop
a handfull of borage. a little spinage. put all these into a pott
& Cover it very Close. lett it stand in a brass pott of boyleing water
3 or 4 houres. then still all these things in a still vntill they
be very deyd. then put a good quantity of red stone sugars or
white. Probatum:

To make gilly of a Capon
Excellent for a Consumption
Take of China the weight of Gd: thin Slices and steep

with it a Capon well dressed. with a hand full of borage

Sorrell. halfe a pound of raysons stoned. of daws sliced
of prunes. 20. of sanders the weight of 8 d. boyle all these
together till they come to aboute 6 pintes n strayne
it and keepe it by it selfe. till it Grow Cold. then add
there to. 12 ounces of refined sugar. powdered. 1/4 of
Cinamon bruised. two spoone fulls of the Inice of almonds
e whites of 2 eggs beaten. lett it run clearely through
a fine cloth. Lastly lett it coole- and it will gelly. of
this lett the party take 4 or 5 spoone fulls at any time
with broth. or of it selfe.

A Aleoieine for the Meagsum in the head, or a Gargae.
Take sage, roemary, and pellitony of Spain,the roowy of each of these
a like quantity, & boyle them in a pint of vinegar upon a chatondish of
coles, unthill halfe bee consumed, then putt in 2 good spoonfull of mustarde
bring made Master good vinegar, & so tel it boyle a while, and then take a little
of it as hott as you can suffer, and hold it in your mouth, as you shall taste
ocasion, & then spit it out, and take more, and thy do five or six times
every morning so long as  you shall fine ocasion, or teele the selfe greived.
A water for the fare
A bottle of water, one ounce of white Mercury, the purest you can get,
bra.. in small between two pages, then putt in to you in an darsthen
siptin, & sett it on the fire, and when it begins to ...., take it of, for
much boyling makes it froth the shin, when it y cold scam it with a
feather, & strayne it through a fine cloth, & pure it from the bottome;
If you can get, let it stand all night after you have boyed it, before you scamme or
straine it will bee the purer, you must take 4 ounces of Plain tai.e water
as much of Strawberry water, as much of brane flower water, & halfe
a pound of damask rose water, mingle them all together. Then take & quart
of a pound of bitter almonds, a quart of a pound of sweet almonds,
blanch them, and pound them altogether, and straine out  the s.eing.
of them with  the mil=ngled water. Then putt 3 quarters of a pound
of venire Carusse well beaten between two pap.y, stirre them
altogether then putt them into the Mercury water in a glasse
bottle shakeing it well altogether for almost halfe an houre,
and when  you use it, you must shake it.

You may take of drye wormwood half a pound
common agua vita 2 quarts fountain water one
quart this you must steep together 24 houres & then
still it till you have a quart or something lesse then
you must take away that which is in the reservoir
which is the spirit of the Aqua vita & smells strong of
the wormwood which must be used thus; you must
take drye wormwood finely powdered 2 ounces
to which you must put for much of that spirit
as will throughly wett the wormwood, you
must stop the glass that none may breath out,
leaving it for, in some cool place for 3 dayes and
then strain it through a fine cloth & then you
shall have a very bitter liquour, which you must use
in wine or beer putting in 3 or 4 drops as you shall
find cause.

A glister for e Collicke in the belly: L. Hu: taught her by
Mr Cooke, and by him approued.
Take a pinte of Muskadine infuse it in an ounce of Annesseedes bruised
an ounce of bayberries husked and bruised, an handfull of Camomile
flowers let theise simper in the wine an hower, but not boile; and if
the Collicke bee extreame & this giue not ease, then giue an other glister
made like this onely add to it ten drops of oile of annesseeds, and
ten drops of spirit of Juniper.
A glister in case of distraction, taught the L: Hu: by
Mr Cooke, and by him approued.
Take sage, Bettony, Sweete Marioram, rosemary tops of each halfe
an handfull of Camomile, Beetes, holyhocks, & vyolet leaues of each
halfe an handfull, of fennellseedes bruised 3. spoonefulls; boile all
theise in a Broathe of a sheepes head, vntill but a pinte & an halfe
remaine, and in a pinte of this decoction dissolue one ounce
of the extract of white Helebore, & twoe spoonefulls of the
syrrupe of vyoletts, mixe theise together, & cast it vp as a glister
of a temperate heate; ether at 5. a clocke in e morneinge, or
at 4. in e afternoone; But this is too rough for weomen in
childbed, because it prouoketh vomittinge very much; but for others
it is a most soueraigne and infallible medicine. /

Take a neso laid egg make a little hole in e topp of itt and lett out all
the white as cleane as you can; then take of the left honey, and

it to wasme in the fire, and if ohere bee any drosse in the honey it
will rise upto the top, wch you must take of with a knife, then sup
of yr egg fasteninge with a draught of beere & aile mingled togrther
with some nuttmegg & sugar therein.
A Medicine for the stone given e L Huby mr. Cooke
and by him often approved
Take the cloves of 3 heades of garlicke, pilled & shread smale, boile
them in a pinte of milke, still if bee sender and all the milke boiled
away; then stampe the garlicke to a pultice; then apply it as hast as
may, bee suffred upon a lynin cloathe to the lower parts, and this
will give ease in futts of the stone, or gravell; to a man apply
befwiset his codds & ffandaments, and to a woman upon the share
bone; and to the beaseinge place.

To cure the Pin and Nebb in the eye. LHu:
Take a pound of clay butter & clarify it in the sunne fiue or
sixe daies together, then straine the clearest of it through
a cloathe into a new earthen Pott well glassed within
then take a good quantity of the hearbe Christopher and
wipe every leafe and shred them very smale; then fill up
the butter soe clarified whilst it is warme with asmuch of
the shrewd hearbes as it will take up, then close up the pott
soe close that noe ayre nor wett may get into it, and bury it
in the ground abbout sixe weekes; then take it up againe and
cast away soe much of it as you finde putrified & melt the
rest upon a Chafingdish of embers in a flatt new earthen Pott
or Pan, but bee sure it boile not; then straine it, put it
into a glasse and keepe it for your use, wch must bee in
this manner Take at a tume asmuch of it as a prettie
Bigg Lins heade & gently annointe the eyes therewith
this





This Medicnie this made will continue good for
seaven yeares.

To helpe a woman frome miscarryinge La H: given
her by Mrs. Harbert, and by her often approued.
Take Oaken budds before they bee blowne, and still them in
a Rose still; and lett the woman take a spoone full or twoe
every morneinge and eveninge if shee use to miscarry
abbout any certaine tyme; the next tyme shee is with
Child, let her begin this water a Moneth before her usuall
tyme of miscarryinge; and drink it for 2 monethes together
and if shee bee ill at any other tyme when shee is with Child
after the twoe monrthes are expired;let her take twoe
spoone full of it at any tyme of the day or night.

FFor a sore throate L: L Hughes: tried by Mrs: Harnbet.
Take a good quantity of the time leave grass stalkes, rootes
and leaves; Cover them with facie water , boile & scam them
let them stand till the water looks red then straine it
through from the hearbes into an earthen Bottle without
an handle, & when the party is to jleit, let the Bottle
on the fire, & pull out the corke stopp the ; and when itt
boules put a wooden Funnell over the Bottle with the little
end unto your mouth sucke up the steame & swallow it downe
and when it hath done reakeinge gargle your throate with the
water the oftener the better, though it shallbee a ..uinsle
take alsoe trooe taggs weeke' your throate, and apply it alsoe
youre eares as hott as you can endure it, and mussle youre selfe up
with a warme drye clotathe.

newest drinke you can gett and put to the hearbes, stirr them
well together and straine them; then put in a Bottle to keepe it
then take halfe a pinte thereof and make it lukewarme, so drinke
it when you goe to bed lyinge as low with your heade as you can, againe
take it in e morneinge fasteinge untill it bee all spent; and if one
quantity doe not cure you make more of it, and use it againe; and
let some of it bee spurtled into yr eye, and in short tyme it will
cure it
ffor a straine theise 3 things followeinge taught the
L Hu: by Mrs Harb:
Take Comphry chop it small, put it into a pinte or more of good Ale
a good handfull of Comfry will settle to this quantity of Ale; twoe
handfull of Ashe of wheatebrann; boile all theise to a pultice
apply it as hott as yu can endure; shift it twice a day.
The second taught her by Mrs Harbert
Take a good quantity of Parsley shread it & put to it a little
white wine; boile it a little and lay it to e place as hott as yu
can endure it; shiftinge it twice a day.
The Third:
Make a Ladder of Castle soape; and bathe e party in it
as hott as you can an hower together
ffor the runinge of the Reynes L Hu taught
her by Mrs Harbert
Take twoe handfull of plantine leaves, one handfull of knotgrathe
(in winter twoe handfulls) one handfull of Camphire rootes,
washed, scraped and thin sliced; put all theise in a Bottle of
small Ale or juice pintes and boile them to a quart; Drinke
a good draught of this in the morneinge fasteinge, and
last at night, continue it a while it soe; and it will
cure you, you must take it warme.
ffor Crablice
Take a melteld cloath, such Siluersmithes use; rubb the place wth
it, and it will kill them.






Ffor the runninge of the raines LadyH: taught her by L. G. M.
Take a new laid egg, take forth the white, warme it upon embers
and fill it up with white orris well sifted and mingled together
let the patient sip it up in the morneinge, takeinge an hour
after at t feast; this maybe taken at any tyme as there is occation
but if the party bee very ill, then it must bee taken constantly for
six dayes together.
To make conserve of Rosemary flowers LCla:
To a powder of Rosemary flowers pick & cleane from the husks take
a pound of sugar finely beaten, as y are beatinge of the flowers
strane it in by degrees amongst them,
Ffor the Ricketts L Hu: taught her and approved
by mr: Bacchus.
Take one ounce of May butter an handfull of Dill, as much of very
of gile off dill and dill of verym of each one ounces, of butter
almonds twoe ounces for all your may butter, dill & sugar aquarose
of them away ; then put in the three oiles and boile it on; then
take crimson Taffata or Sarcenet, and cover it over both Canvases
downe, halfe an ounce of cloves, twoe Nutmeggs, twoe spoonefulls
of Grains of wheate breade; some franekincense & masticks
Beate all theise so well; then mingle them together with the
Crums & straine them upon the Taffata; and savedest, then
cover it over againewith downe, and quit it thick with silks
and for let the Child neare it as a Cap; when y annointe the
Child you must stroake downe his eyes every morneinge and be
sure hee takes noe cow; and in the three hot, and in the three
all monethes hee must not bee annointed; Give him sometyme
a quarter of a drink of new Cow milkwith red rose water and
Sugar; and swinge him betweene y hands sixe or seven tymes away.
To recover Children from Consumption fitts L Hu
Open of the hare that growes upper most upon a Beares back
a prettie quantity thereof; boile it on aquawater for halfe
an houer; then in t eveninge father t Chicks feete with it as hott
as hee can endure it; then take t hve soe boiled in t aqua with a
and lace it; to y soles of his feete, and this done once
or twice will doe much good; if not cure it.

Another for the same. L Hu:
Take three piomie seedes broken into powder, and putt
them into a spoonefull of Blacke cherrie water, and guie it to
the childe; and with a spoonefull or twoe of the water wash it downe
yu must giue it three daies before the change of the Moone, and
three daies after, and at any other tyme when you porceuie the
child is like to have a fitt.
Another for the same L Hu: taught her by e LStapleton
On Midsommerday before sunrise gather the dew of white thornes
shakeinge it into a Bason soe much as will seene that yeare; take
flower and make dough of it with a little of the dewe, and make
a Cake thereof without any other Ingredients except Currants;
laie it on the harthstone, and the party is to eate the quantitie
if they can untill the fitts cease wch willbe in a short tyme.

It helpeth alsoe inflamations.

A Bath for the Scuruey L Hu: taught	
her by Mrs Halbert, and tryed.
Take sixe handfull of Mallowes, twoe handfull of coastmary
twoe handfull of Lauander leaves, twoe handfull of Bay leaves
a pecke of wheate bran; deiude the bran into three
Bolter Baggs, tye them not too close, and boile them with
the hearbes in faire water, soe make a bathe, and sitt
therein as longe as you will.
A scuruey Ale. L Hu: taught her by Mr Clayton.
Take a good handfull of scuruey grasse, of brooke lyme and
water Cresses of each, an handfull, one ounce of ffennell
seedes or annes seedes, a quarter of an handfull of wormewood
Beate all theise together in a Morter; and take the first






Hurstice of them and make a little loafe there of with
meale; take it in an Oven, and put the loafe hott, cutt
in ffower pooeds into sine gallons of good Ale; let it stand
tooe or three daies and then drink there of both in the
morneinge, aftermoone and at your pleasure
Avery good Pultied for a sore breast L Hu
quien her by Hu and approved.
Take a quart of milk, put therem an handfull of brook tyme
an handfull of Camomile, as much dryed roses, thread them
very smale with tooe or three spoone fulls of lyndeede, boile them
altogether well; then thicken it with wheate flower, boile it
well after, and when e take it of the fire, put in an handfull
of Mustton surtt shreade very smalle; stirr it well together, and
lay it on as warme as ecan suffer it; shift mge it troied a
day, and it will aire the breast.

A Caudle for weakness, and unoderate fleasee of
whites Sradds, queen the L Hu: by  Clayton.
Take halfe a pinte of Maskadine, and ounce of good white
sugar candy beaten smale, of Canamon mead muttmeggs &
singer of each one drachme shred halfe of them, and pound
the other halfe smale; then put them all in a skillett wth
the Muskadine; then stake fower dates, Cutt them in halfes
the soones and pith picks out shred them, and pound or brake
them smale with a little Miskadine; then straine them and beate
the yolkes of tooe nesoland eggs with a little miskadine
then straine the yolks, and straskollett with the Muskadine
and spices over the fire, let it seeth before  yo putt in the
date; then put in the eggs and take it from the fire; and
take tooe spoonefull of it first in a morneinge before
yogoe to bed bifr ovet of bed, and last at night when
yo goe to bed





For an Agne L Hu
Take myne rootes of yarrowe bruise them and dry
them that they offend not the stomach; then make a
little Bagg prportion able to the Pitt of the stomache
quelt the yarrow into it and hange it vpon the pitt of the
stomach; or boile red sage in possett drinke and take a
good draught of it an hower before the fitt comes.
To make grlly flower sallet L H. by your L B.
Take as much white wine venegar as white wine boile them
and a proportionable quantity of grlly flowers in ythe liquo
vntill they bee tendor just good store of sugar to them; and
let them haue liquod enough.
A glister of the bloody flue L H by Mr Cooke
Take the wice of yarrow leaues a quarter of apinte, halfe soe
much wice of planbine, and a quarter of a pinte of newe
milk; dissolue in this as much mutton suett as an egg
and lett it bee cast or as a glister, and euery day one of thiese
made of a temperate heale, for 2 or 3 daies together
well much all aile.
Another for the same by the same.
Take sixe handfull of wheate brann and three hanfull
of lack Chalke finely seraped; dry them well ouer the
frie in a pan; then put them into a lynen cloath or bagg
and apply them warme to the belly; let it lye on all night
itt will all aile you much.
To make werinegara L H taught herby Mr. Wraughton
Take sixe quarters of wine vinegar, putt it in glasse
and fill the glasse with elder budds halfe full, set it in
the sun close stopped for the space of a moneth
To make rasberry vinegar L H by Mr. Wraughton
Take sixe quarters of white wine vinegar, and fower
quarters of rasberries, picke them cleane; put them in an earthen pott all night close couerd in the
moreinge straine the vinegar from ythe rasberries, and
putt twoe quarters of fresh rasberries into ythe pan
and





and so let it stand all theyeare, and when you straine
the vinegar put it into a greate glasse bottle, and lett it
stand a Monnth in the sunne.
A medicine for the smole poxe, for  fiteigne of a
mad dog, & for the new dissease. L. H:
Take a quart of stronge Ale one handfull of hearbe of greece
one handfull of sage, three heades of garlicke, fower peny=
worth of treackle being halfe a pound, one spoonefull of
pewter scraped, boill all shiyle in a Pupkin couered and
close stopt with past, that the steame may not come out till
halfe bee wasted.
ffor the smale poxe, strucken in
you may giue suze spoonefulls at once for twoe or three
seuerall tymes as you see caused, or oftener, if you finde the
party in danger
ffor the fiteinge of a Madd dogg
you are to giue sixe spoonefulls of this water atone tyme
for three morneings together fasteinge, next after
fittinge; and for three moorneings together after the full
of the Moone, and chaunge followeinge.
And alsoe for the new dissease or malignant feauer
use it iust in the same manner
To caue a Child to make water L Hu: taught
by L. B.
Take the powder of an egg shell dryed in the ouev; giue it
the child you it will helpe him; or take the powder of
stone take as much as will lye upon a groate; giue in possett
drinke made of white wine and it will presently helpe
or take an handfull of smallgoge, fry it in butter, apply it to
 Belly or Nauell of the Patient as hott as hee can sufferr it
let it lye on for twoe howers, and it will helpe uery much.
To staie loosenesse L Hu: taught herby
Doctr. Enderlaine.
When a Hare is new kill saue the blood in a cleane lynen
swath, drye it, and hang it in a peece of this cloath in a bottle
ether of Beare or wine; let the party drinke hereof till his
loosenesse bee stained; wh willbee in a short tyme.

For the wynde of the Mother of Mastrise
L.Hu: taught her by Dr. Underlane
Take of bayberries , huske them, beat them to powder and mingle sugar
with them accordinge to your tasts in clary water, wherein boill
dissolve a little gumme draggon, boil that till they bee ready
to dropp; and when they are cold, drop twoe or three dropps of
oil of nuttmeggs upon a Cake thereof ; and  we eate  one
in the mornings, and the other at night, the oil is
best to bee present out and drachme is quantity
enough!
A. H.





A Medicine for the toothache L: Hu: by
Valentine Pell.
Take ginger, liquorice, Alicompane roote dryed, & Cynamon of
theise fowre a like quantity (that is a drachme) nutmeggs, Maie
and cloues a like quantity  (that is a scruple) beate all theise
spices into fine powder, searce them through a fine suie,
and mixe them all well together; putt some of it into a quill
and blowe it up into the nostrills nearest to the paine; and
it will give perfect helpe.

spoonefull thereof.

To make an oile of a red dogg that cured a lame, drye &
withered Arme perfectly; wch had beene soe 12. yeares L. H
Take a younge red haired dogg, keepe him three daies wth out
meate, then strangle him with a Cord, let him lye deade a
quarter of an hower, in the meane tyme boile a Kettle
of sallett oile upon the fire, and put in the dogg, it makes noe
greate matter whether hee bee whole or cutt in peeces soe
that hee bee all there, skin and haire; seeth him
almost to peeces, keepe the Kettle alwais close covered
and





and whilest it is boileinge, take 4 store or an hundred
scorpions, and put them in a bason on the fire intill they bee
througly burnt, then put them into the kettle with the oile & dogg
putt there unto a good dishfull of greate gorund roomes or smalladge
well washed a good handfull of ... Johns nort, an handfull of roilde
or Marsh Mallowes, one handfull of Mallowes worth an ounce of
saffron seeth alltogether roell ontill the flesh of the dogg be broken
and fallen pooeds; and because which must have much oile, you may
at first put in halfe into the kettle trooe parts of water, and
but one of oile, and in seetheinge powrel in water till the dogg
bee almost broken in pooeds as abbouesain;  then ... it wake cold
after this take the bones of the dogg and the hearbes; and when
they have pressed and squeeezed them wrill that the substance
remaines in the said oile; then cast them away, then take that
onely wich is abbove the water the oile & greate, and cast away
the water if there bee much of it; but if there bee soe little water
asyo.. can searsily dis coone the oile from the water, you shale
take altogether for a little water cannott bee good; then
straine it through a strainer or Canuces; first wett and
steeped in white wine; then takes ... or eight ounces
of on guenstum ..., a pound of the marrow of the
gammon, and bones of an hogg, a pound or more ipyon
can gett it of the marrowe of the nones of the hinder
partes of thighes of an Asse; Putt all theise together
with the said oile and grease serth if ... pon the fise
then add to it a dishfull or an halfe of the oile of roses
and when it seetheth you shall putt there to three ounces of
masticke, three ounces of gain ellemy eight ounces of
red wake; but the masticke and gaine must obee will beaten
into powder, and sifted; and when all thes hath boiled
halfe an hower, lett it coole againe, and set it in the sunn
in some close vessell couered for 7 or ... daies, and
you shall have an one collent substance and water for all
ruide of cold enfirmities, and for many of the sal soe.
Take Author hero of did see the experience of it upon a
gentlemans left Arme that was lesse then the other
by





By the one halfe, and hee had nether strength in it, nor any
use thereof to helpe himselfe with; and beinge annointed
with this oile (in the sunne) by the space of an hower together
untill the oile was cleane dryed up: and had alsoe pearred
through his Arme, within 3 daies beinge; it was
perfectly discemied that the raines gave nourishment
unto the members, and within nyne daies after, that arme
was as full of fleshe as the other arme; and as Sounde & whole
as if it had never beene hurt.

Another for the Same tryed by mySelfe upon Dr:
Harton & Sr. ron Kinadon, when Speech was lost
yet recouered.
Take the spirit of pure venice Turpentine, Siauen tymes
drawne, and twoe or three drops thereof give at a tyme in white
whyne or Ale twice or thryce a day for a weeke together will
restore you againe; and if the palsie take any other wrinse
then rubb that jointe with this spirit, chafeinge it with a
warme hand for a quarter of an hower together; and itt
will psently restore it againe

Putt all them into a posset in this nanner followinge.

A drinke against the plague. L. H: by D. Clayton
Take three pintes of malmsey or muskedin, boile therein
an handfull of sage with soe much rue untill a pinte
bee wasted awaie; then straine it and lett it over the fire
againe; & putt there to a drachme of longe pepper halfe
an ounce of ginger, with a quarter of an ounce of nutmeggs
well beaten together; let it boile a little, and put there to
two drachmes of Methridate, and one drachme of ...
a quarter of a pinte of Dougon water or Aquetifa
or Angelico water; Reepe this water as a most
soveraigne abbove all of hers; take of it alwaies
mornieinge





morneinge and eveninge three spoonefulls at a tyme
if the party bee alreadie infected; if not one every moon=
rnge is sufficeint, in all plague tymes next to god must your
trusye in this water as most indoubted and to helpe you
ffor though it haue beene tryed upon many, and it neuer failed
any if the heart were not dead and drowned with the dull al
before this was taken.
 To duleyer a woman of a dead childe. L adyHughes:
taughts herby Dr. Clayton
Take amber, beate it to powder and make a fume ouer coales
lett the woman putt ouer it with chrathes and Blancketts clow
albout her for some halfe an hower and give her to drinke
some Amber in bitches milk; it may chaunci to take
away her senses; especially abbout the tyme when the fume
and srink meebe; put in short tyme it oprnrth the body
and shee willee duleyered; This was tryed by one Mrs
Brinkhurst of Backinghamshire upon a gentle wooman
in greate extremily and danger of her life; because it was
truely syprcted that the childe was deade in her body
whereupon this was giuen & done unto her; and thereupon
within halfe an hower shee was in greate extremtie and
speecheless; but was instantly duliudeel well & safe; sayeing
that abbout the very tyme the fume and drink melt
shee felt her body open as it were with ascrue.

L. Jlu: taught her by Mrs. J Les.

red rosh water; in facse all their thengs together fowre
and torn by horoers; then distill them in a Leinbeche
and dreanke forore spoonefull of it at once
The vertues of it are their siaucen especially

The wall nutt water for the outues there of called
the water of life: LJLU: taught her by Mrs. JLLS
Take wallnutts in the begennige of June, beate them in a
Moter & still them in a still of leade, roope that water by
it selfe, then gather more wallnutts about Midsomer day
and the them as you did the other, and rope y water by
it selfe alsoe: then abbout a fortnight after that, gather
more wallnutts, and use them as you did both y former
roepeige  the water alsue by it selfe; then take a quart
of each water, and putt it altogether, and distill
it





it in a stillitory of glasse of glasse, and keepe it close stopt
for your use.
The vertues of it are theise followeinge.
i. It heules all manner of dropsies and palsies, druncke with
white wine fasteinge.
2. It is good for the eyes, if you drop one dropp into them
3. It is good to helpe the conception of weomen, if shee drinke mo=
deratly thereof one spoonefull a day in white wine.
4. It will make ones face uery faire, if it bee washed therewth.
5. It is good for infirmities within the body, and druieth out of it
all corruption, if it bee drunke moderatly wth wine or water.
6. It killeth wormed in the body, ether drunke with wine, or by it
selfe.
7. If wine have turned or chaunged his coulor, put a little vyall
hereof into it, and within 4 daies it will rectify it againe.
Whosoe drunketh of this continually shall liue soe longe as
nature may well continue in him.

of Goates blood L H: by mr. Cooks, and  glister to bee
taken before the use of it
Take every morneinge fastinge seven spoonfulls of the

doe this other seaven morneings together, then for beare 20.
daies, then come to t use again both of the quintessence of
goats blood for seaven morneings and after that of the Bean shell
water againe, whereof y shall find an admirable effecte
but before y make use of it the day before make use of this
glister followeinge.

To stopp bleedinge of a wound at the nose L.Hu: taught her by Bancrofte Bishopp of Oxford:
Take a lynen cloath, wash it in white wine vinegar, then dry it wash it soe a second tyme then dry it againe,
and in a be tender of it; spreade this in a cutt or wound & it will presently stoppoyolent bleedeinge; or
put it up into the nostrills and it will stopp bleedeinge at the nose; the ... of the hearbe perry
winckle put up witha fine ungg into the nose workes the same effect;
soe ... doth ether veriliu or vinegarr used soeether with a ragg or linte.
Another for the same the samee
Take the shaveings of bleu leather paint, put it up into thenosorills and it will staunch bleedinge at the nose.
For the scurvey L.Hu: taught her by the L Bacelues.
Take a Bashell of Malt make thereof twelue gallons of Ale when you turn it up hange it in a Bagg with a quarter of  a pound of varsons of the sunn stoned, twoe ounces of anneseedes bruised,
one ounce of Orrange Pills; if you please a good hand full of roman ... dryed, as much best any as much sage both dryed;which sage is to bee bouled in the Bort, and when the turn up your
Ale put there unto a pinte of the ... of water cresses, as much the mice of Seurvey grasse, and when it is eight or tenn daies of you may drinke it with your meat or at any tyme.
Ffor the Kings evill. L.Hu:
Gather halfe a Bushell that is fower gallons of the fflowers of ffore gloves cleane picks and sifted beate.





204
Beate them very smale and rinse them with as
much fresh butter very cleane washt from the churne
as the foxe flowers can drink up; then put them into
a new Pipkin that was never used, and sort them over a
gentle fire, and lett it boile softly abbout fouer howers
then take it of, and when it is cold cover it closed, one in a
Moneth sett the pipkin in an oven after the breede is
dran one; puttinge into it a peled off freshe butter to keep
it from groweinge nought; and when your use it lye avery
fine hollan cloath on the sore; then take for much of this
ointement in a saweor as will some for once & warme it
and dye the fine cloathe in it & lett it bye upon the sore
till it bee well; if the sore bee broken it will make it runne
very much at the first; the first springe and fall after
the sore is well, take some purgeinge dryringe phisicke
such as Phisiteans shall thincke best agree with e body
A Drink for the stone L Hu
Make a Bagg of a yard of fallen double, then take
Mallowes, holeyoaks, parsley, fennell, clary, sasifrage
white Archangell of each an hand full, eight topps of
Speare Mint, wash the heartes cleane, & dry them in a cloathe
take Callames, Aromatius and gallengall of each e waight of
twelue ponces, of gum arrabeck, qum troagantha, gum of
a cherre tree and myrrh the best, of each the waight of
sixe pones lardge Mace one ounce, of Anneseed as twoe
ounces, bruse all theise, and put them and the heartes in yo
bagg, tyeinge it closed; then put into it sixe ponses of the
best white wine, let it boile with a quick fire tilll twoe pints
there of bee consumed; then powre it out into a Bason
and lett it stand till it bee soe cold that you may wringe the
bagg; then put to it three quarters of a pound of fine hard
sugar, and when its cold bottle it up for youse; drink
every moremge sixe greate spoonefullls of it for three
daies together, restinge from it other three daise
and soe for three weeks every springe & fall
To make





To make the womans Balsom to helpe deliuery and
other uertues, & how to use it, galen L Hby Mr Coche.
Take fowre handfull of the purest white garden lillies, shread
them and infuse them in sixe ounces of the sweetest & best
Oile Omphasinum, wch is of greene Olives, and let all theise
stand in e sun fowre daies.; then straine it, & infusee like
quantity of lillies, and lett them stand in the sunn sixe daies
in the meane tyme gett three ounces of the oile of sweete
Almonds new drawone without fire, & mingle it with the
foresaid oile of lillies; then mingle theise together, and
dissolue it in twelue graines of the best Civett, wch must
bee kept together in a glass, close stopt for yo use, in this
manner followeinge a Moneth together before e  tyme of
the womans deliuery, lett all her belly on both sides to her
graines, and soe farr as her belly hes abboue her nauell bee
annointed with this balsom with a warme hand before
five a quarter of an hower together; and first it will
keepe all the vaines oher belly from danger of breakeinge
although shee should haue uery hard labour; and next it will
prepare her body against the tyme of her labour, that shee
will haue speedy and good labour; and with lesse paine & trouble
ffor a sore legg or other part L H
taught her by Doctor Clayton
Take vergin waxe, & oile Olive of each a like quantity , boile
them in water, shaueingethe wax into the Oile; & keepe this
ointement for your use.
ffora sore legg burneinge of scabb. by the same
Take Maloine that hath broade woody leaues and yellowe

make a plaester hereof & lay it to thefore legg; Mr. Richard
Qiftorden Hampshre cured with this a gentlewoman and sore
legg; wch had beene given ouer by divers good Chirargeons
as uncuraeable

of the oile with all the waxe cutt in smale peeces, and when

itt for your old sore/ theise bee the verturs./
1. It cures old sores, & greene wounds applyed there to plaisterwise/
2. If mitigates the paine of any Aches, applyed searce cloathe Coise
3. If cures Imposticines in e face, ffellons, & giues present remedy to those

4. If yu pase cornes after e full Moone very neare for that which fetch noe

bee whole.
Sr. Gilber Talbotts water & powder to cure a wounde
Taught hereby Sr. Tho Alesbury.
Take a quart of a pound of green or bleue vitrioll beaten
dissolve it in a quart of water, in some of this water putt
any ragg that hath the bloode of the wounded party
upon it, keepe it in a Pot indifferent warme & where
wash ... the wounde ether with white wine or whey
& keepe it uery cleane./

A Balsome & the vertues thereof  L H
Take a quarter of a pound of waxe cutt small, put it
with a pinte of sacke into an earthen pott, melt it
take it of the fire, and add to it halfe a pound of veriuce

lett





lett it coole throughly, then take the Cake met it it againe
then putt in an ounce of red saunders & stirr it continually till it bee cold.
The vertues of this Balsome are theise.
1. It cures greene  woundes, melted in a spoone & applyed.
2. It cures burneings & sealdings beinge there wch annointed.
3. It cures outward bruises ; applyed outwardly.
4. It cures all inward bruises, taken warme with sacke or possett
drinke, the quantity of an hazell Nutt.

An oile for an Ache L H: app: by Lady North
Take twoe handfull of Camomile, as much greene sage, as much fetherne=
wood, as much wormewood, some topps of Lavender, twoe handfull of red=
mose leaves: Put these into a gallon of sallett oile; they must
stand nyne daies how steepe therein, and bee stirred every mornige
and after nyne boile them upon embers 2. howers, stirr them
still, when they hath boiled an hower put in a quantity of aqua
composita, when it hath boiled 2. howers straine it through a cloath
when it is cold put it up into a glass;All theyo hearbes must bee
gathered in May whene dew is not upon them the Cam: sage, and
fethernewood must bee of an equall waight, the herbes must bee
cutt smale before they bee putt into the oile ye must have halfe a pinte
of aqua compostita and by little & little once in a quarter of an hower
whilest it is in boill enigh it must bee outt into it, the rose leaves and
lavender must bee pulled and cutt before they bee putt into ye rest; this oile
must bee chafed in with warme hand ether before the fire, or bee a
little warmed when you please/.

Rabes, and soe lay it as a pultice to the sore; if you have

Take virga aurea , wch is a golden rod, wormewood, Baglass, mugwort

It is good also for woomans breastes, and putrified bones makinge
them to seale and for aches in the stomacke. Also to break an
Imposthume, makinge it to come out ; it hath drain bulletts

place of the sore; its good to staunch bloode

Take May Butter and Neatesfoote oill of each twoe

Searet, mixe them together  ouer the fire, and lett

To make an ordinary Sysant L H
Take a pottle of Ale, a quarter of a pound of raisons of the sunne
stoned, nyne or ten good figgs, three sticks of liquorice

bruised, and a little bgg of braune; put all theise in a neice
Pipkin close couered, boile it on a soft fire till one halfe
bee consumed, then straine it, and sweeten it with some
white sugar candy, or white sugar accordinge to your taste
and for take three or forore spoonefulls of it at a tyme.

A very good water for any wound or sore LH
Take a quarter of a pound of Orange Pills dryed & beaten into fine
powder, a bottle of runninge water that runs from norther
South, boile the Pills in the water fower of fiue howers
then strane squish it throngh a fine cloth without squeeszing
it to a brogde preserueinge glasse; then after a fortnight
tyme when it is fully settled and all the Mother down taken
out, put it up in a cleane glass & keepe it for you the so keepe
bee in the manner, wash the wounde of sore with it then dipp
apron of fine lyner cloathe into the water and and apply it
and if it bee an old sore  often  wash it hepe with
the better.

To make the best pomatum Le tu: taught her by
Mris Jles
Take the leafe of a Barrowhogg, laie it in runninge
water 4 daies, shifteinge the water eury day; then take
the suett and putt it inot a deepe gally pott, and lett it into
a skillet of water ouer the fire, lettinge it stande till it
bee all melted, then straine into a bason of faire water
and lett it stand foe bottom away, then cutt it out, and it there
bee any lett leinge ini the muddle of the cake, serape it of and
dry it in a cleane cloathe, then put it into a a gally pot againe
Lettinge it on the fire as before, lastinge it onely in elt, and lean
in if there be any neede; then powre in a good quantity
of rose water, and when you have taken it of the fire
leafe it up, ether with a longe spoone or a sticke, till it
bee stone cold and it must stand for a day or twoe; then cutt
it out of the pott, and pwore owt of the rose water, seraye
The Cake the second tyme it ovation bee; and dry itt on
a cleane cloathe as before; then putt into the pott againe
as before; adn melt and pwore in annother  good quantity
of Rose water, and beate it till it bee cold; after it hath
stood thus a day or twoe, cutt it out, dry it uery well from
the water, otherwise it will not keepe; then melt it
and powre it into little potts; you neede make of this
but twice in the yeare, when you are to use it
steepe guine dragon in rose water, and to halfe a
pound of this suett, add the quantity of a good
Walnutt of that gum dragon; and incorporate
both well together into a morter; and for use
it, as you haue ou ation

A bask for one with begins to hauee Propsie Mr Boureker
Take with hearbes & flowers of Camomile & handfull of small agge
and Lauander seauen handfulls, of hysope, wormewood, fether
fewe and sage of each  2 good handeful; seeth them in faire
water, put them in a tubb, let them coole of them selues wthout
puttinge any cold water to them; then twice a week bath
therein; take for e most part most meate; for moyst
meate engendreth waterysh humors and encreaseth
the dropsie; Drinke stale beare, or gaseoigne wine
a draught at euery meale; hott meates and Rootes must
bee sodden in his broathes; if your belly swell annointe
it with the oile of Camomile, or any hott oile; drinke
not abboue a quart in one day; and sd bee not so lulled
take some sena in pottadge or broathe
Another approued Medicine for e dropsie Mr. Bourcher
Take the longe spriggs that growes upon the stalks of
greene broome, burne it in an Ouen clearne scrapt when you
have as much Ashes of it as a peny loafe, mixe them with a
good quantity of white wine; stir them well together, and
lett them stand twoe daies; then straine e wine from your
Ashes and night and morneinge, drinke half a pinte
thereof, and when thatis gone mashe more the same
way; Drinke it as aforesaid; and in ashuint tyme
you will bee cured perfectly

To knowe whether the dropsy bee cureable or noe M Bounher
Take Barley Meale, theise of spinage & Marygold
make a plaister thereof, and lay it three tymes to the
Patients Liuer; and if the plaister bee soone drye, they
are cureable; if otherwise incureable.

Another for the Droppe Mr. Bouch.
Take an handfull of the tender topps of Broome stampe and
straine them in a quart of stale Ale, boill it till halfe
bee consumed; putt some Cenamon and ginger to it, sett the
Patient upright in a Chaire; soe that his feete touch not e
ground by twoe foote and an halfe, then put under his feete
a pan of Charcoale; and over  e pan laie a paire or twoe of
tongues; and lay thereon greene broome; let the patient sett
the soles of his feete thereon; and give him some of that Ale
to drinke bloode warme; lett him sweate well; and putt
him to bed; and by gods grace it will helpe him in a
short tyme

Labor to helpe Delivery. L Hu:
Take a Pecke of lillies, picke the Leaves, shreade

therewith; it will much ease them, & asswadge
swellinge; and is good for weomen in Labour to
helpe Delivery.

To make the Queens Mother of ffrance her Pomatum
Mr Cooke taught it the La: ffn:
Take sheeps feete abbout e full Moone, Seal of the haire & be

and it is a most pure pomatum.

To make the greene oile for all Bruises in May ...
Take cammomile, tops of bays, fether few, sage, reroses
the whites dipt of Balme, of each twoe handfulls
sothernwood, elder budds, Lavander, Lavander Cotton, Rue,
Rosemary, Costemary, Buglasse, rounter savery, elme leaves
of each one handfull, shred c smale, & put in a glasse
put in as much sallett oile as will cover them, then tye up
the glasse close, sett it in the sunne for 3. or 4. weeks
stir it every day with a stick; then cruise some 30.
cloves and put into it; take yo glasse close tyed, & sett it
in a skellett; then fill it up with waber, & lett it boile a
day upon a soft fire; always e water boiles awaie loe putt
in more; when it is boilled straine it, and put sixe
spoone full of clove oile to it, stopp it closet, & rope it
for yo use. The rertue here of id for all old or
now bruises, to bee warmed & annointe yo bruised
part morneinge & eveninge; oftner forcation bee
it may bee takn unoard upon any fall or bruise in the
body 3. spoonefull warmed in adraught of white wine
or possess drink.
